→  Fueron acusados de conspirar para cometer fraude.

CONSTERNATION   /ˌkɒnstərˈneɪʃən/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la consternación

Meaning:  a feeling of anxiety or dismay, usually caused by something unexpected

Example:  The news was met with consternation by the staff.

→  La noticia fue recibida con consternación por el personal.

CONTRAVENE   /ˌkɒntrəˈviːn/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  contravenir; infringir

Meaning:  to act in a way that breaks a law, rule, or agreement

Example:  The company's actions contravened the new regulations.

→  Las acciones de la empresa contravinieron las nuevas normativas.

CONTRIVANCE   /kənˈtraɪvəns/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el artificio; el artilugio

Meaning:  a clever device or plan, sometimes seen as artificial or unnatural

Example:  The plot twist felt like a bit of a contrivance.

→  El giro argumental se sintió un poco artificial.

CONUNDRUM   /kəˈnʌndrəm/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el enigma; el dilema

Meaning:  a confusing and difficult problem or question

Example:  Finding affordable housing remains a conundrum for many families.

→  Encontrar vivienda asequible sigue siendo un dilema para muchas familias.

CONVALESCE   /ˌkɒnvəˈlɛs/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  convalecer

Meaning:  to gradually recover health and strength after an illness

Example:  She spent two weeks convalescing at her parents' house.

→  Pasó dos semanas convaleciendo en casa de sus padres.

CORDIAL   /ˈkɔːrdʒəl/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  cordial

Meaning:  warm and friendly, though somewhat formal

Example:  The two leaders had a cordial meeting.

→  Los dos líderes tuvieron una reunión cordial.

CORROBORATE   /kəˈrɒbəreɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  corroborar

Meaning:  to confirm or support a statement or theory with evidence

Example:  Two witnesses corroborated her account of the accident.

→  Dos testigos corroboraron su relato del accidente.

COVERT   /ˈkoʊvərt/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  encubierto/a; secreto/a

Meaning:  done secretly, not openly

Example:  The agency ran a covert operation for months.

→  La agencia llevó a cabo una operación encubierta durante meses.

CREDENCE   /ˈkriːdəns/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la credibilidad

Meaning:  belief or acceptance that something is true

Example:  The new evidence gives more credence to her story.

→  Las nuevas pruebas dan más credibilidad a su historia.

CRYPTIC   /ˈkrɪptɪk/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  críptico/a; enigmático/a

Meaning:  having a hidden or unclear meaning

Example:  He left a cryptic message on my phone.

→  Me dejó un mensaje críptico en el teléfono.

CULMINATION   /ˌkʌlmɪˈneɪʃən/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la culminación

Meaning:  the highest point or final result of a process

Example:  The award was the culmination of years of hard work.

→  El premio fue la culminación de años de duro trabajo.

DEBASE   /dɪˈbeɪs/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  degradar; envilecer

Meaning:  to reduce the quality, value, or dignity of something

Example:  He felt the comment debased his achievements.

→  Sintió que el comentario degradaba sus logros.

DECREE   /dɪˈkriː/  •  noun; verb  •  C1

Spanish:  el decreto; decretar

Meaning:  an official order with the force of law; to officially order something

Example:  The government issued a decree banning the practice.

→  El gobierno emitió un decreto que prohibía la práctica.

DECRY   /dɪˈkraɪ/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  denunciar; condenar públicamente

Meaning:  to publicly criticize something strongly

Example:  Activists decried the new policy as unfair.

→  Los activistas denunciaron la nueva política como injusta.

DEFER   /dɪˈfɜːr/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  posponer; deferir

Meaning:  to postpone something; to accept someone else's opinion out of respect

Example:  They decided to defer the decision until next month.

→  Decidieron posponer la decisión hasta el próximo mes.

Common:  defer to

DEFLECT   /dɪˈflɛkt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  desviar

Meaning:  to cause something to change direction; to avoid dealing with something directly

Example:  She deflected the question with a joke.

→  Ella desvió la pregunta con una broma.

DEFT   /dɛft/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  hábil; diestro/a

Meaning:  showing skill and quickness, especially with the hands

Example:  With a deft movement, she fixed the wire.

→  Con un movimiento hábil, ella arregló el cable.

DEMEANOR   /dɪˈmiːnər/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el comportamiento; la actitud

Meaning:  the way a person behaves or appears to others

Example:  She kept a calm demeanor throughout the interview.

→  Ella mantuvo una actitud tranquila durante toda la entrevista.

Note:  British spelling: "demeanour".

DEMISE   /dɪˈmaɪz/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la desaparición; el fallecimiento

Meaning:  the end or failure of something; death

Example:  The rise of streaming led to the demise of video rental stores.

→  El auge del streaming provocó la desaparición de las tiendas de alquiler de vídeos.

DENOUNCE   /dɪˈnaʊns/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  denunciar

Meaning:  to publicly criticize or condemn something as wrong

Example:  The leader denounced the attack as an act of terrorism.

→  El líder denunció el ataque como un acto de terrorismo.

DEPOSE   /dɪˈpoʊz/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  destituir; deponer

Meaning:  to remove a leader from power, often by force

Example:  The general was deposed after the coup.

→  El general fue depuesto tras el golpe de estado.

DEPRECATION   /ˌdɛprɪˈkeɪʃən/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la desaprobación

Meaning:  strong disapproval of something

Example:  He spoke with self-deprecation about his early failures.

→  Habló con autocrítica sobre sus primeros fracasos.

DERIDE   /dɪˈraɪd/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  ridiculizar; burlarse de

Meaning:  to mock or ridicule someone or something

Example:  Critics derided the film as unoriginal.

→  Los críticos ridiculizaron la película por poco original.

DESPISE   /dɪˈspaɪz/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  despreciar; detestar

Meaning:  to dislike someone or something intensely

Example:  She despises dishonesty in any form.

→  Ella detesta la deshonestidad en cualquier forma.

DESPONDENT   /dɪˈspɒndənt/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  desanimado/a; abatido/a

Meaning:  feeling very sad and without hope

Example:  He felt despondent after losing the match.

→  Se sintió abatido después de perder el partido.

DESPOT   /ˈdɛspɒt/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el déspota

Meaning:  a ruler with unlimited power, especially one who uses it cruelly

Example:  The novel is set under the rule of a cruel despot.

→  La novela transcurre bajo el gobierno de un déspota cruel.

DETACHMENT   /dɪˈtætʃmənt/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el desapego; la indiferencia

Meaning:  a lack of emotional involvement; the state of being separate

Example:  She spoke about the accident with surprising detachment.

→  Ella habló del accidente con una indiferencia sorprendente.

DEVASTATE   /ˈdɛvəsteɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  devastar

Meaning:  to destroy something completely; to cause severe emotional shock

Example:  The news of his death devastated the whole community.

→  La noticia de su muerte devastó a toda la comunidad.

DIATRIBE   /ˈdaɪətraɪb/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la diatriba

Meaning:  a forceful and bitter verbal attack against someone or something

Example:  He launched into a diatribe against modern technology.

→  Se lanzó a una diatriba contra la tecnología moderna.

DICHOTOMY   /daɪˈkɒtəmi/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la dicotomía

Meaning:  a division between two things that are opposite or entirely different

Example:  There's a clear dichotomy between his public and private life.

→  Hay una clara dicotomía entre su vida pública y privada.

DIGRESS   /daɪˈɡrɛs/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  divagar; desviarse del tema

Meaning:  to move away from the main topic when speaking or writing

Example:  Sorry, I keep digressing — let's get back to the main point.

→  Perdón, sigo divagando; volvamos al punto principal.

DISCERNING   /dɪˈsɜːrnɪŋ/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  perspicaz; exigente

Meaning:  showing good judgment, especially about quality

Example:  The restaurant caters to a discerning clientele.

→  El restaurante atiende a una clientela exigente.

DISCOURSE   /ˈdɪskɔːrs/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el discurso; el diálogo

Meaning:  communication in speech or writing, especially formal discussion of a topic

Example:  Public discourse around the issue has become more civil.

→  El diálogo público en torno al tema se ha vuelto más cortés.

DISCREDIT   /dɪsˈkrɛdɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  desacreditar

Meaning:  to harm someone's reputation or make people doubt their claims

Example:  The scandal discredited the entire organization.

→  El escándalo desacreditó a toda la organización.

DISDAIN   /dɪsˈdeɪn/  •  noun; verb  •  C1

Spanish:  el desdén; despreciar

Meaning:  a feeling of superiority and contempt for someone or something

Example:  He treated the offer with disdain.

→  Trató la oferta con desdén.

DISPARAGE   /dɪˈspærɪdʒ/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  menospreciar; denigrar

Meaning:  to speak of someone or something in a critical, dismissive way

Example:  He never disparages other people's work.

→  Él nunca menosprecia el trabajo de los demás.

DISPARATE   /ˈdɪspərət/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  dispar; diverso/a

Meaning:  very different from each other, so not able to be compared

Example:  The committee brings together disparate views on the issue.

→  El comité reúne opiniones dispares sobre el tema.

DISSONANCE   /ˈdɪsənəns/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la disonancia

Meaning:  a lack of harmony or agreement; an unpleasant combination of sounds

Example:  There's a certain cognitive dissonance in his argument.

→  Hay cierta disonancia cognitiva en su argumento.

DISSUADE   /dɪˈsweɪd/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  disuadir

Meaning:  to persuade someone not to do something

Example:  Her parents tried to dissuade her from quitting school.

→  Sus padres intentaron disuadirla de dejar la escuela.

Common:  dissuade from

DIVULGE   /dɪˈvʌldʒ/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  divulgar; revelar

Meaning:  to make known private or secret information

Example:  She refused to divulge her sources.

→  Ella se negó a divulgar sus fuentes.

DOCILE   /ˈdɒsəl/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  dócil

Meaning:  easy to control or teach; quiet and obedient

Example:  The horse was surprisingly docile despite its size.

→  El caballo era sorprendentemente dócil a pesar de su tamaño.

DUPLICITY   /duːˈplɪsəti/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la duplicidad; el engaño

Meaning:  deceitful behavior that hides your true intentions

Example:  His duplicity was finally exposed after the investigation.

→  Su duplicidad finalmente quedó al descubierto tras la investigación.

ECCENTRIC   /ɪkˈsɛntrɪk/  •  adjective; noun  •  C1

Spanish:  excéntrico/a

Meaning:  unconventional and slightly strange in behavior or appearance

Example:  The old professor was known for being eccentric.

→  El viejo profesor era conocido por ser excéntrico.

ECLIPSE   /ɪˈklɪps/  •  noun; verb  •  C1

Spanish:  el eclipse; eclipsar

Meaning:  an event when one celestial body blocks another; to make something seem less important by comparison

Example:  His early success was eclipsed by later scandals.

→  Su éxito temprano quedó eclipsado por escándalos posteriores.

EGREGIOUS   /ɪˈɡriːdʒəs/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  flagrante; atroz

Meaning:  outstandingly bad; shocking

Example:  It was an egregious error that cost them the contract.

→  Fue un error flagrante que les costó el contrato.

ELUCIDATE   /ɪˈluːsɪdeɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  aclarar; elucidar

Meaning:  to make something clear by explaining it in detail

Example:  Could you elucidate your point a bit further?

→  ¿Podrías aclarar tu punto un poco más?

EMANCIPATE   /ɪˈmænsɪpeɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  emancipar

Meaning:  to free someone from legal, social, or political restrictions

Example:  The reform emancipated thousands of workers from unfair contracts.

→  La reforma emancipó a miles de trabajadores de contratos injustos.

EMBODIMENT   /ɪmˈbɒdimənt/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la encarnación; la personificación

Meaning:  a person or thing that represents an idea or quality perfectly

Example:  She's the embodiment of grace under pressure.

→  Ella es la personificación de la elegancia bajo presión.

EMPIRICISM   /ɪmˈpɪrɪsɪzəm/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el empirismo

Meaning:  the belief that knowledge comes mainly from experience and observation

Example:  His philosophy is grounded in empiricism.

→  Su filosofía se basa en el empirismo.

EMULATE   /ˈɛmjəleɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  emular

Meaning:  to try to match or copy someone's achievements or behavior

Example:  Young players try to emulate their favorite athletes.

→  Los jóvenes jugadores intentan emular a sus atletas favoritos.

ENDOWMENT   /ɪnˈdaʊmənt/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la dotación; el patrimonio

Meaning:  a gift of money given to fund an institution; a natural talent

Example:  The university received a large endowment from a former student.

→  La universidad recibió una gran dotación de un antiguo estudiante.

ENIGMA   /ɪˈnɪɡmə/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el enigma

Meaning:  a person or thing that is mysterious or difficult to understand

Example:  To many, he remains something of an enigma.

→  Para muchos, él sigue siendo todo un enigma.

ENSHRINE   /ɪnˈʃraɪn/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  consagrar; plasmar

Meaning:  to preserve a right or idea in a form that ensures it is respected

Example:  These rights are enshrined in the constitution.

→  Estos derechos están consagrados en la constitución.

ENTICE   /ɪnˈtaɪs/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  atraer; tentar

Meaning:  to attract someone by offering something appealing

Example:  The discount enticed many new customers.

→  El descuento atrajo a muchos clientes nuevos.

EPITOME   /ɪˈpɪtəmi/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el epítome; la personificación

Meaning:  a perfect example of a particular quality or type

Example:  She's the epitome of professionalism.

→  Ella es la personificación del profesionalismo.

EQUANIMITY   /ˌiːkwəˈnɪməti/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la ecuanimidad

Meaning:  calmness and composure, especially in a difficult situation

Example:  She accepted the criticism with equanimity.

→  Ella aceptó la crítica con ecuanimidad.

ERUDITE   /ˈɛrədaɪt/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  erudito/a

Meaning:  having or showing great knowledge gained from study

Example:  He gave an erudite lecture on ancient philosophy.

→  Dio una conferencia erudita sobre filosofía antigua.

ESOTERIC   /ˌɛsəˈtɛrɪk/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  esotérico/a

Meaning:  understood by only a small number of people with special knowledge

Example:  The book covers some fairly esoteric mathematical theories.

→  El libro trata teorías matemáticas bastante esotéricas.

ESPOUSE   /ɪˈspaʊz/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  adoptar; abrazar (una causa)

Meaning:  to support or adopt a belief, cause, or way of life

Example:  She espouses a minimalist lifestyle.

→  Ella adopta un estilo de vida minimalista.

EUPHEMISM   /ˈjuːfəmɪzəm/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el eufemismo

Meaning:  a mild or indirect word used instead of a harsh or unpleasant one

Example:  "Passed away" is a common euphemism for "died".

→  "Falleció" es un eufemismo común para "murió".

EXACERBATE   /ɪɡˈzæsərbeɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  exacerbar; agravar

Meaning:  to make a problem or situation worse

Example:  The drought exacerbated the region's food shortage.

→  La sequía agravó la escasez de alimentos en la región.

EXASPERATE   /ɪɡˈzæspəreɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  exasperar

Meaning:  to make someone very irritated or frustrated

Example:  His constant excuses exasperated his manager.

→  Sus constantes excusas exasperaron a su jefe.

EXEMPLAR   /ɪɡˈzɛmplər/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el ejemplo modelo; el paradigma

Meaning:  a person or thing that serves as a typical or ideal example

Example:  The building is an exemplar of modern architecture.

→  El edificio es un ejemplo modelo de la arquitectura moderna.

EXEMPLARY   /ɪɡˈzɛmpləri/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  ejemplar

Meaning:  serving as a desirable model; excellent

Example:  Her exemplary behavior earned her a promotion.

→  Su comportamiento ejemplar le valió un ascenso.

EXIGENCY   /ˈɛksɪdʒənsi/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la exigencia; la urgencia

Meaning:  an urgent need or demand, especially in a difficult situation

Example:  The exigencies of the situation required a quick decision.

→  Las exigencias de la situación requerían una decisión rápida.

EXPEDITE   /ˈɛkspɪdaɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  agilizar; acelerar

Meaning:  to make a process happen more quickly

Example:  We paid extra to expedite the delivery.

→  Pagamos un extra para agilizar la entrega.

EXPUNGE   /ɪkˈspʌndʒ/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  eliminar; borrar (por completo)

Meaning:  to completely remove something, especially from a record

Example:  His criminal record was expunged after ten years.

→  Su historial delictivo fue eliminado después de diez años.

EXTORT   /ɪkˈstɔːrt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  extorsionar

Meaning:  to obtain money or something else through force or threats

Example:  He was accused of trying to extort money from the business.

→  Fue acusado de intentar extorsionar dinero de la empresa.

EXTRADITE   /ˈɛkstrədaɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  extraditar

Meaning:  to hand over a person accused of a crime to another country for trial

Example:  The suspect was extradited to face trial.

→  El sospechoso fue extraditado para enfrentar el juicio.

EXUBERANCE   /ɪɡˈzuːbərəns/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la exuberancia; el entusiasmo desbordante

Meaning:  a feeling of great happiness and energy

Example:  The children's exuberance filled the whole house.

→  El entusiasmo desbordante de los niños llenaba toda la casa.

FABRICATE   /ˈfæbrɪkeɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  inventar; fabricar (una mentira)

Meaning:  to invent false information; to manufacture something

Example:  He fabricated the entire story to avoid trouble.

→  Inventó toda la historia para evitar problemas.

FACADE   /fəˈsɑːd/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la fachada

Meaning:  the front of a building; a false appearance hiding the truth

Example:  Behind her cheerful facade, she was deeply worried.

→  Detrás de su fachada alegre, estaba profundamente preocupada.

FALLACY   /ˈfæləsi/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la falacia

Meaning:  a mistaken belief, especially one based on unsound reasoning

Example:  It's a common fallacy that money always brings happiness.

→  Es una falacia común que el dinero siempre trae felicidad.

FERVOR   /ˈfɜːrvər/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el fervor

Meaning:  intense and passionate feeling about something

Example:  She spoke about the cause with real fervor.

→  Ella habló de la causa con verdadero fervor.

Note:  British spelling: "fervour".

FICKLE   /ˈfɪkəl/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  voluble; inconstante

Meaning:  changing frequently, especially in loyalty or opinion

Example:  Fashion trends can be quite fickle.

→  Las tendencias de moda pueden ser bastante volubles.

FIDELITY   /fɪˈdɛləti/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la fidelidad

Meaning:  faithfulness to a person, cause, or belief; accuracy

Example:  The recording captures the sound with great fidelity.

→  La grabación capta el sonido con gran fidelidad.

FLAGRANT   /ˈfleɪɡrənt/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  flagrante

Meaning:  very obvious and shocking, especially breaking a rule

Example:  That was a flagrant violation of the rules.

→  Eso fue una violación flagrante de las normas.

FLOUT   /flaʊt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  desafiar; incumplir abiertamente

Meaning:  to openly disregard a rule or law

Example:  The company flouted safety regulations for years.

→  La empresa incumplió abiertamente las normas de seguridad durante años.

FOMENT   /foʊˈmɛnt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  fomentar (conflicto); instigar

Meaning:  to stir up or encourage trouble, unrest, or violence

Example:  The rumors were designed to foment distrust.

→  Los rumores estaban diseñados para fomentar la desconfianza.

FORTITUDE   /ˈfɔːrtɪtuːd/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la fortaleza; la entereza

Meaning:  courage and strength shown in facing pain or adversity

Example:  She faced the illness with remarkable fortitude.

→  Ella enfrentó la enfermedad con notable entereza.

FRATERNIZE   /ˈfrætərnaɪz/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  fraternizar; confraternizar

Meaning:  to associate in a friendly way with others

Example:  Employees are discouraged from fraternizing with clients.

→  Se desaconseja a los empleados fraternizar con los clientes.

Note:  British spelling: "fraternise".

FUROR   /ˈfjʊrər/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el furor; el escándalo público

Meaning:  an outbreak of public anger or excitement

Example:  The comment caused a furor on social media.

→  El comentario causó un gran escándalo en las redes sociales.

Note:  British spelling: "furore".

GALVANIZE   /ˈɡælvənaɪz/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  galvanizar; impulsar a la acción

Meaning:  to suddenly encourage someone to take action

Example:  The speech galvanized the crowd into action.

→  El discurso impulsó a la multitud a la acción.

Note:  British spelling: "galvanise".

GARNER   /ˈɡɑːrnər/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  cosechar; obtener (apoyo, atención)

Meaning:  to gather or collect something, such as support or attention

Example:  The film garnered critical acclaim.

→  La película cosechó elogios de la crítica.

GLOSS   /ɡlɒs/  •  noun; verb  •  C1

Spanish:  el brillo; disimular

Meaning:  a shiny surface; to make something seem better than it is by ignoring the negatives

Example:  He tried to gloss over the company's financial problems.

→  Intentó disimular los problemas financieros de la empresa.

Common:  gloss over

GRAVITAS   /ˈɡrævɪtɑːs/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la seriedad; la gravedad (de carácter)

Meaning:  dignity and seriousness of manner

Example:  The judge spoke with real gravitas.

→  El juez habló con verdadera seriedad.

GULLIBLE   /ˈɡʌləbəl/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  crédulo/a; ingenuo/a

Meaning:  easily tricked or deceived because of being too trusting

Example:  He's gullible enough to believe anything he reads online.

→  Es tan crédulo que se cree cualquier cosa que lee en internet.

HAMPER   /ˈhæmpər/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  obstaculizar; dificultar

Meaning:  to hinder or prevent progress

Example:  Bad weather hampered the search efforts.

→  El mal tiempo obstaculizó los esfuerzos de búsqueda.

HAPLESS   /ˈhæpləs/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  desafortunado/a; desdichado/a

Meaning:  unlucky, especially in a way that seems amusing

Example:  The hapless intern spilled coffee on the boss's desk.

→  El desafortunado becario derramó café sobre el escritorio del jefe.

HEDONISM   /ˈhiːdənɪzəm/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el hedonismo

Meaning:  the belief that pleasure is the most important thing in life

Example:  The resort promotes a lifestyle of pure hedonism.

→  El complejo promueve un estilo de vida de puro hedonismo.

HEED   /hiːd/  •  verb; noun  •  C1

Spanish:  hacer caso a; atender; la atención

Meaning:  to pay attention to advice or a warning

Example:  You should heed your doctor's advice.

→  Deberías hacer caso al consejo de tu médico.

HEGEMONY   /hɪˈdʒɛməni/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la hegemonía

Meaning:  leadership or dominance of one group or country over others

Example:  The empire established hegemony over the region.

→  El imperio estableció su hegemonía sobre la región.

HIATUS   /haɪˈeɪtəs/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la pausa; el paréntesis

Meaning:  a temporary pause or break in an activity

Example:  The band went on hiatus after their world tour.

→  La banda hizo una pausa tras su gira mundial.

HINGE   /hɪndʒ/  •  verb; noun  •  C1

Spanish:  depender de; la bisagra

Meaning:  to depend entirely on a particular factor; a device that connects a door to its frame

Example:  The whole plan hinges on getting enough funding.

→  Todo el plan depende de conseguir suficiente financiación.

Common:  hinge on

HOMAGE   /ˈhɒmɪdʒ/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el homenaje

Meaning:  a public show of respect or honor toward someone

Example:  The film pays homage to classic horror movies.

→  La película rinde homenaje a las películas de terror clásicas.

Common:  pay homage to

HOMOGENIZE   /həˈmɒdʒənaɪz/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  homogeneizar

Meaning:  to make things the same or similar throughout

Example:  Globalization has homogenized many aspects of culture.

→  La globalización ha homogeneizado muchos aspectos de la cultura.

Note:  British spelling: "homogenise".

HUMILITY   /hjuːˈmɪləti/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la humildad

Meaning:  the quality of not being proud or arrogant

Example:  She accepted the award with great humility.

→  Ella aceptó el premio con gran humildad.

IDIOSYNCRASY   /ˌɪdiəˈsɪŋkrəsi/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la idiosincrasia; la peculiaridad

Meaning:  a peculiar habit or characteristic of a person

Example:  Everyone has their own little idiosyncrasies.

→  Todo el mundo tiene sus pequeñas peculiaridades.

IGNOMINY   /ˈɪɡnəmɪni/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la ignominia; el deshonor

Meaning:  public shame or disgrace

Example:  He suffered the ignominy of losing his job publicly.

→  Sufrió la ignominia de perder su trabajo públicamente.

IMPASSE   /ˈɪmpæs/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el punto muerto; el impasse

Meaning:  a situation where no progress is possible; a deadlock

Example:  The negotiations reached an impasse.

→  Las negociaciones llegaron a un punto muerto.

IMPEDE   /ɪmˈpiːd/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  impedir; obstaculizar

Meaning:  to delay or prevent someone or something by obstructing them

Example:  Heavy traffic impeded our progress.

→  El tráfico intenso obstaculizó nuestro avance.

IMPETUS   /ˈɪmpɪtəs/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el ímpetu; el impulso

Meaning:  something that encourages a process or activity to develop faster

Example:  The award gave her career a new impetus.

→  El premio dio un nuevo impulso a su carrera.

IMPINGE   /ɪmˈpɪndʒ/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  afectar; interferir en

Meaning:  to have a negative effect on something; to intrude on someone's rights

Example:  The new rule impinges on our privacy.

→  La nueva norma interfiere en nuestra privacidad.

Common:  impinge on

IMPRUDENCE   /ɪmˈpruːdəns/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la imprudencia

Meaning:  a lack of caution or good judgment

Example:  His financial imprudence led to serious debt.

→  Su imprudencia financiera lo llevó a graves deudas.

INCENSE   /ɪnˈsɛns/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  enfurecer; indignar

Meaning:  to make someone very angry

Example:  The decision incensed local residents.

→  La decisión indignó a los residentes locales.

INCITE   /ɪnˈsaɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  incitar

Meaning:  to encourage someone to act violently or unlawfully

Example:  He was accused of inciting violence at the rally.

→  Fue acusado de incitar a la violencia en el mitin.

INCONGRUITY   /ˌɪnkənˈɡruːəti/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la incongruencia

Meaning:  the state of not fitting in or matching the surrounding context

Example:  There's a strange incongruity between his words and his actions.

→  Hay una extraña incongruencia entre sus palabras y sus acciones.

INDEFATIGABLE   /ˌɪndɪˈfætɪɡəbəl/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  incansable

Meaning:  never getting tired, especially of an activity

Example:  She's an indefatigable campaigner for animal rights.

→  Ella es una activista incansable por los derechos de los animales.

INDIGNATION   /ˌɪndɪɡˈneɪʃən/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la indignación

Meaning:  anger caused by something perceived as unfair

Example:  There was widespread indignation over the decision.

→  Hubo indignación generalizada por la decisión.

INDOCTRINATE   /ɪnˈdɒktrɪneɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  adoctrinar

Meaning:  to teach someone to accept a set of beliefs uncritically

Example:  The regime tried to indoctrinate young students.

→  El régimen intentó adoctrinar a los jóvenes estudiantes.

INDOMITABLE   /ɪnˈdɒmɪtəbəl/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  indomable

Meaning:  impossible to defeat or discourage

Example:  She has an indomitable spirit despite her illness.

→  Ella tiene un espíritu indomable a pesar de su enfermedad.

INEFFABLE   /ɪnˈɛfəbəl/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  inefable; indescriptible

Meaning:  too great or extreme to be expressed in words

Example:  There was an ineffable sense of peace on the mountain.

→  Había una sensación inefable de paz en la montaña.

INERTIA   /ɪˈnɜːrʃə/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la inercia

Meaning:  a tendency to stay the same or not act, out of habit or laziness

Example:  Sometimes inertia keeps people in jobs they don't enjoy.

→  A veces la inercia mantiene a la gente en trabajos que no disfruta.

INGRATIATE   /ɪnˈɡreɪʃieɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  congraciarse

Meaning:  to try to gain someone's favor by flattering or pleasing them

Example:  He tried to ingratiate himself with the new manager.

→  Intentó congraciarse con el nuevo gerente.

Common:  ingratiate oneself with

INNOCUOUS   /ɪˈnɒkjuəs/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  inocuo/a; inofensivo/a

Meaning:  not harmful or offensive

Example:  It seemed like an innocuous comment, but it upset her.

→  Parecía un comentario inocuo, pero la molestó.

INSIDIOUS   /ɪnˈsɪdiəs/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  insidioso/a

Meaning:  causing harm gradually and without being noticed

Example:  The disease has an insidious effect on the body over time.

→  La enfermedad tiene un efecto insidioso en el cuerpo con el tiempo.

INSINUATION   /ɪnˌsɪnjuˈeɪʃən/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la insinuación

Meaning:  an indirect and often unpleasant suggestion

Example:  He was offended by the insinuation that he had lied.

→  Se ofendió por la insinuación de que había mentido.

INSTIGATOR   /ˈɪnstɪɡeɪtər/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el instigador; la instigadora

Meaning:  a person who starts or encourages an action, especially trouble

Example:  Police identified him as the instigator of the fight.

→  La policía lo identificó como el instigador de la pelea.

INTREPID   /ɪnˈtrɛpɪd/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  intrépido/a

Meaning:  fearless and adventurous

Example:  The intrepid explorers crossed the desert alone.

→  Los intrépidos exploradores cruzaron el desierto solos.

INTROSPECTION   /ˌɪntrəˈspɛkʃən/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la introspección

Meaning:  the examination of your own thoughts and feelings

Example:  The long trip gave her time for introspection.

→  El largo viaje le dio tiempo para la introspección.

INUNDATE   /ˈɪnəndeɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  inundar

Meaning:  to overwhelm someone with too many things at once; to flood

Example:  We were inundated with applications for the position.

→  Nos vimos inundados de solicitudes para el puesto.

INUNDATION   /ˌɪnənˈdeɪʃən/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la inundación; el aluvión

Meaning:  a large flood, or an overwhelming amount of something

Example:  The office received an inundation of complaints.

→  La oficina recibió un aluvión de quejas.

JARGON   /ˈdʒɑːrɡən/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la jerga

Meaning:  special words or expressions used by a particular group or profession

Example:  The contract was full of legal jargon.

→  El contrato estaba lleno de jerga legal.

JEOPARDIZE   /ˈdʒɛpərdaɪz/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  poner en peligro

Meaning:  to put something at risk of harm or failure

Example:  One more mistake could jeopardize the whole project.

→  Un error más podría poner en peligro todo el proyecto.

Note:  British spelling: "jeopardise".

JUBILATION   /ˌdʒuːbəˈleɪʃən/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el júbilo; la alegría desbordante

Meaning:  a feeling of great happiness and triumph

Example:  There was jubilation in the streets after the victory.

→  Hubo júbilo en las calles tras la victoria.

JUXTAPOSITION   /ˌdʒʌkstəpəˈzɪʃən/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la yuxtaposición

Meaning:  the placing of two contrasting things close together for effect

Example:  The film uses juxtaposition of past and present scenes.

→  La película utiliza la yuxtaposición de escenas del pasado y el presente.

LACONIC   /ləˈkɒnɪk/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  lacónico/a

Meaning:  using very few words to express something

Example:  His laconic reply gave nothing away.

→  Su respuesta lacónica no reveló nada.

LAMENT   /ləˈmɛnt/  •  verb; noun  •  C1

Spanish:  lamentar; el lamento

Meaning:  to express deep sorrow or regret about something

Example:  She lamented the loss of the old neighborhood.

→  Ella lamentó la pérdida del antiguo barrio.

LANGUISH   /ˈlæŋɡwɪʃ/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  languidecer

Meaning:  to remain in an unpleasant situation for a long time without improvement

Example:  The proposal has been languishing in committee for months.

→  La propuesta lleva meses languideciendo en comité.

LATITUDE   /ˈlætɪtuːd/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la libertad de acción; la latitud (geografía)

Meaning:  freedom to act as you choose; the distance from the equator

Example:  She was given a lot of latitude to make her own decisions.

→  Se le dio mucha libertad para tomar sus propias decisiones.

LEGITIMIZE   /lɪˈdʒɪtɪmaɪz/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  legitimar

Meaning:  to make something legal or acceptable

Example:  The vote helped legitimize the new government.

→  La votación ayudó a legitimar el nuevo gobierno.

Note:  British spelling: "legitimise".

LETHARGY   /ˈlɛθərdʒi/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el letargo

Meaning:  a lack of energy and enthusiasm

Example:  The heat left everyone in a state of lethargy.

→  El calor dejó a todos en un estado de letargo.

LIAISON   /liˈeɪzɒn/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el enlace; la coordinación

Meaning:  communication or cooperation between people or groups

Example:  She acts as a liaison between the two departments.

→  Ella actúa como enlace entre los dos departamentos.

LIBERATE   /ˈlɪbəreɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  liberar

Meaning:  to set someone free from confinement or oppression

Example:  The city was liberated after years of occupation.

→  La ciudad fue liberada tras años de ocupación.

MALIGN   /məˈlaɪn/  •  verb; adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  difamar; calumniar; maligno/a

Meaning:  to say unpleasant and untrue things about someone; harmful

Example:  She felt she had been unfairly maligned in the press.

→  Sintió que había sido difamada injustamente en la prensa.

MANIFESTO   /ˌmænɪˈfɛstoʊ/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el manifiesto

Meaning:  a public declaration of policies and aims, especially political

Example:  The party published its manifesto ahead of the election.

→  El partido publicó su manifiesto antes de las elecciones.

MARGINALIZE   /ˈmɑːrdʒɪnəlaɪz/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  marginar

Meaning:  to treat a person or group as unimportant or push them to the edges of society

Example:  The policy risks marginalizing rural communities.

→  La política corre el riesgo de marginar a las comunidades rurales.

Note:  British spelling: "marginalise".

MASQUERADE   /ˌmæskəˈreɪd/  •  verb; noun  •  C1

Spanish:  hacerse pasar por; la mascarada

Meaning:  to pretend to be something you are not

Example:  He masqueraded as a doctor for years.

→  Se hizo pasar por médico durante años.

Common:  masquerade as

MAXIM   /ˈmæksɪm/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la máxima

Meaning:  a short statement expressing a general truth or rule of behavior

Example:  "Honesty is the best policy" is an old maxim.

→  "La honestidad es la mejor política" es una vieja máxima.

MENDACITY   /mɛnˈdæsəti/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la mendacidad; la falsedad

Meaning:  the habit of lying

Example:  The scandal exposed years of corporate mendacity.

→  El escándalo expuso años de mendacidad corporativa.

METAPHOR   /ˈmɛtəfɔːr/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la metáfora

Meaning:  a word or phrase applied to something it doesn't literally describe, to suggest a comparison

Example:  The poem uses the sea as a metaphor for life.

→  El poema usa el mar como metáfora de la vida.

METTLE   /ˈmɛtəl/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el temple; el coraje

Meaning:  a person's ability to cope well with a difficult situation

Example:  The crisis really tested her mettle.

→  La crisis puso a prueba su temple.

MIMIC   /ˈmɪmɪk/  •  verb; noun  •  C1

Spanish:  imitar; el imitador

Meaning:  to copy someone's behavior, speech, or appearance, often for humor

Example:  She's great at mimicking different accents.

→  Ella es genial imitando diferentes acentos.

MISCONCEPTION   /ˌmɪskənˈsɛpʃən/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la idea equivocada; el concepto erróneo

Meaning:  a belief that is wrong or based on faulty thinking

Example:  It's a common misconception that bats are blind.

→  Es un concepto erróneo común que los murciélagos son ciegos.

MOBILIZE   /ˈmoʊbəlaɪz/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  movilizar

Meaning:  to organize people or resources for a particular purpose

Example:  The organization mobilized volunteers after the earthquake.

→  La organización movilizó a voluntarios tras el terremoto.

Note:  British spelling: "mobilise".

MOLLIFY   /ˈmɒləfaɪ/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  apaciguar; aplacar

Meaning:  to calm someone who is angry or upset

Example:  The manager tried to mollify the angry customer.

→  El gerente intentó apaciguar al cliente furioso.

MUNIFICENT   /mjuːˈnɪfɪsənt/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  munificente; generoso/a

Meaning:  very generous, especially in giving

Example:  The museum received a munificent donation from an anonymous patron.

→  El museo recibió una donación munificente de un mecenas anónimo.

MYSTIQUE   /mɪˈstiːk/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el misterio; la mística

Meaning:  an air of mystery and fascination surrounding a person or thing

Example:  The old castle still has a certain mystique about it.

→  El viejo castillo todavía tiene cierto misterio.

NEFARIOUS   /nɪˈfɛriəs/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  nefasto/a; malvado/a

Meaning:  wicked or criminal

Example:  The film's villain has nefarious plans for the city.

→  El villano de la película tiene planes malvados para la ciudad.

NEGATE   /nɪˈɡeɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  anular; negar

Meaning:  to cancel out the effect of something; to deny the existence of something

Example:  One mistake can negate months of hard work.

→  Un error puede anular meses de trabajo duro.

NEMESIS   /ˈnɛməsɪs/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la némesis; el archienemigo

Meaning:  a person's greatest rival or a cause of their downfall

Example:  The hero finally faced his nemesis in the final scene.

→  El héroe finalmente se enfrentó a su némesis en la escena final.

NEPOTISM   /ˈnɛpətɪzəm/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el nepotismo

Meaning:  favoritism shown to relatives or friends, especially in giving jobs

Example:  The company was criticized for nepotism in its hiring.

→  La empresa fue criticada por nepotismo en sus contrataciones.

NONCHALANCE   /ˌnɒnʃəˈlɑːns/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la despreocupación; la indiferencia

Meaning:  a casual lack of concern or interest

Example:  He answered the difficult question with surprising nonchalance.

→  Respondió a la pregunta difícil con una despreocupación sorprendente.

NULLITY   /ˈnʌləti/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la nulidad

Meaning:  the state of being legally invalid or void

Example:  The court declared the contract a nullity.

→  El tribunal declaró la nulidad del contrato.

OBFUSCATE   /ˈɒbfəskeɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  ofuscar; oscurecer (a propósito)

Meaning:  to make something unclear or confusing, often deliberately

Example:  The report seemed designed to obfuscate the real numbers.

→  El informe parecía diseñado para ofuscar las cifras reales.

OBLIVION   /əˈblɪviən/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el olvido

Meaning:  the state of being completely forgotten, or unaware of what's happening

Example:  The band faded into oblivion after their last album.

→  La banda cayó en el olvido tras su último álbum.

OBSTINATE   /ˈɒbstɪnət/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  obstinado/a; terco/a

Meaning:  stubbornly refusing to change your mind or approach

Example:  He remained obstinate despite everyone's advice.

→  Él siguió siendo terco a pesar del consejo de todos.

ODYSSEY   /ˈɒdəsi/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la odisea

Meaning:  a long and eventful journey or experience

Example:  Their trip across three continents was a real odyssey.

→  Su viaje por tres continentes fue toda una odisea.

OMEN   /ˈoʊmən/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el presagio; el augurio

Meaning:  a sign believed to predict a future event, good or bad

Example:  They considered the black cat a bad omen.

→  Consideraron al gato negro un mal presagio.

ONUS   /ˈoʊnəs/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la responsabilidad; la carga

Meaning:  a duty or responsibility placed on someone

Example:  The onus is on the company to prove its claims.

→  La responsabilidad recae en la empresa de probar sus afirmaciones.

ORTHODOXY   /ˈɔːrθədɒksi/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la ortodoxia

Meaning:  traditional or widely accepted beliefs, especially religious or political

Example:  His ideas challenged the economic orthodoxy of the time.

→  Sus ideas desafiaron la ortodoxia económica de la época.

OSTENTATION   /ˌɒstɛnˈteɪʃən/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la ostentación

Meaning:  a showy display meant to impress others

Example:  The wedding was a display of pure ostentation.

→  La boda fue una muestra de pura ostentación.

OSTENTATIOUS   /ˌɒstɛnˈteɪʃəs/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  ostentoso/a

Meaning:  designed to impress others through an obvious display of wealth

Example:  He drives a rather ostentatious sports car.

→  Él conduce un coche deportivo bastante ostentoso.

OSTRACIZE   /ˈɒstrəsaɪz/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  marginar; hacer el vacío a

Meaning:  to deliberately exclude someone from a group

Example:  She was ostracized by her classmates after the incident.

→  Sus compañeros de clase la marginaron después del incidente.

Note:  British spelling: "ostracise".

OVERSHADOW   /ˌoʊvərˈʃædoʊ/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  eclipsar

Meaning:  to make someone or something seem less important by comparison

Example:  Her achievements were overshadowed by her sister's fame.

→  Sus logros fueron eclipsados por la fama de su hermana.

OVERTONE   /ˈoʊvərtoʊn/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el matiz; la connotación

Meaning:  an additional meaning or feeling suggested but not stated directly

Example:  His comment had racist overtones.

→  Su comentario tenía connotaciones racistas.

PACIFY   /ˈpæsɪfaɪ/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  apaciguar; pacificar

Meaning:  to calm someone down or restore peace

Example:  He tried to pacify the crying child with a toy.

→  Intentó apaciguar al niño que lloraba con un juguete.

PARADIGM   /ˈpærədaɪm/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el paradigma

Meaning:  a typical example or model of how things are done

Example:  The discovery represents a new paradigm in medicine.

→  El descubrimiento representa un nuevo paradigma en la medicina.

PARAGON   /ˈpærəɡɒn/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el paradigma; el modelo de virtud

Meaning:  a person who is a perfect example of a particular quality

Example:  She's regarded as a paragon of honesty.

→  Se la considera un modelo de honestidad.

PARALYZE   /ˈpærəlaɪz/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  paralizar

Meaning:  to make someone unable to move or a system unable to function

Example:  The strike paralyzed the entire transport network.

→  La huelga paralizó toda la red de transporte.

Note:  British spelling: "paralyse".

PARITY   /ˈpærəti/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la paridad; la igualdad

Meaning:  the state of being equal, especially in status or amount

Example:  The company aims for pay parity between men and women.

→  La empresa busca la paridad salarial entre hombres y mujeres.

PATHOS   /ˈpeɪθɒs/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el patetismo; el pathos

Meaning:  a quality that evokes sadness or sympathy

Example:  The scene was filled with pathos.

→  La escena estaba llena de patetismo.

PERMEATE   /ˈpɜːrmieɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  impregnar; permear

Meaning:  to spread throughout something completely

Example:  A sense of unease permeated the whole meeting.

→  Una sensación de inquietud impregnaba toda la reunión.

PERNICIOUS   /pərˈnɪʃəs/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  pernicioso/a

Meaning:  having a harmful effect, often in a gradual or subtle way

Example:  Social media can have a pernicious influence on self-esteem.

→  Las redes sociales pueden tener una influencia perniciosa en la autoestima.

PERPLEX   /pərˈplɛks/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  desconcertar; dejar perplejo/a

Meaning:  to confuse someone greatly

Example:  The strange results perplexed the scientists.

→  Los extraños resultados dejaron perplejos a los científicos.

PERSONA   /pərˈsoʊnə/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la personalidad pública; la imagen

Meaning:  the image or character a person presents to the world, which may differ from their true self

Example:  Her stage persona is very different from how she is in real life.

→  Su personalidad en el escenario es muy diferente de cómo es en la vida real.

PERSPICACITY   /ˌpɜːrspɪˈkæsəti/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la perspicacia

Meaning:  the ability to understand things quickly and accurately

Example:  She showed great perspicacity in spotting the flaw in the plan.

→  Ella mostró gran perspicacia al detectar el fallo en el plan.

PERVADE   /pərˈveɪd/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  impregnar; extenderse por

Meaning:  to spread through and be noticeable in every part of something

Example:  A feeling of sadness pervaded the entire film.

→  Una sensación de tristeza impregnaba toda la película.

PINNACLE   /ˈpɪnəkəl/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la cúspide; el pináculo

Meaning:  the highest point or peak of achievement

Example:  Winning the award was the pinnacle of her career.

→  Ganar el premio fue la cúspide de su carrera.

PLACATE   /ˈpleɪkeɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  aplacar; apaciguar

Meaning:  to make someone less angry or upset

Example:  He offered a discount to placate the unhappy customer.

→  Ofreció un descuento para aplacar al cliente insatisfecho.

PLURALITY   /plʊˈræləti/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la pluralidad; la mayoría relativa

Meaning:  a large number or variety of something; the largest number of votes, without an absolute majority

Example:  The candidate won a plurality of the votes.

→  El candidato ganó una mayoría relativa de los votos.

POISE   /pɔɪz/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la compostura; el aplomo

Meaning:  a calm and confident manner

Example:  She handled the difficult question with great poise.

→  Ella manejó la pregunta difícil con gran compostura.

POLEMIC   /pəˈlɛmɪk/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la polémica; el discurso polémico

Meaning:  a strong verbal or written attack on someone's ideas or opinions

Example:  His book is a polemic against modern consumer culture.

→  Su libro es una polémica contra la cultura consumista moderna.

PONDER   /ˈpɒndər/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  reflexionar sobre; ponderar

Meaning:  to think carefully about something for a long time

Example:  She sat by the window, pondering her next move.

→  Se sentó junto a la ventana, reflexionando sobre su próximo movimiento.

PRAGMATIC   /præɡˈmætɪk/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  pragmático/a

Meaning:  dealing with things in a practical way rather than following theory

Example:  We need a pragmatic solution, not an ideal one.

→  Necesitamos una solución pragmática, no una ideal.

PRECARIOUS   /prɪˈkɛriəs/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  precario/a

Meaning:  not securely fixed or stable; likely to fail or fall

Example:  Many workers are in a precarious financial situation.

→  Muchos trabajadores están en una situación económica precaria.

PRECIPITATE   /prɪˈsɪpɪteɪt/  •  verb; adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  precipitar; precipitado/a

Meaning:  to cause something to happen suddenly; done too hastily

Example:  The scandal precipitated his resignation.

→  El escándalo precipitó su dimisión.

PRECURSOR   /prɪˈkɜːrsər/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el precursor; el antecesor

Meaning:  a thing that comes before another and often leads to it

Example:  This device was an important precursor to the modern smartphone.

→  Este dispositivo fue un importante precursor del smartphone moderno.

PREDISPOSE   /ˌpriːdɪˈspoʊz/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  predisponer

Meaning:  to make someone likely to behave or think in a certain way

Example:  Genetics can predispose people to certain illnesses.

→  La genética puede predisponer a las personas a ciertas enfermedades.

PREEMPT   /priˈɛmpt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  anticiparse a; prevenir

Meaning:  to take action to prevent something before it happens

Example:  She preempted his objections by explaining her reasoning first.

→  Ella se anticipó a sus objeciones explicando primero su razonamiento.

PREFIGURE   /priːˈfɪɡjər/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  prefigurar; anticipar

Meaning:  to be an early sign or version of something that happens later

Example:  These early sketches prefigure his later famous paintings.

→  Estos primeros bocetos prefiguran sus famosos cuadros posteriores.

PRESAGE   /ˈprɛsɪdʒ/  •  verb; noun  •  C1

Spanish:  presagiar; el presagio

Meaning:  to be a warning sign that something is going to happen

Example:  The dark clouds presaged a storm.

→  Las nubes oscuras presagiaban una tormenta.

PRETENSE   /ˈpriːtɛns/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el pretexto; la pretensión; el fingimiento

Meaning:  an attempt to make something false appear true

Example:  He gained access to the building under false pretenses.

→  Consiguió acceso al edificio con falsos pretextos.

Note:  British spelling: "pretence".

PRODIGY   /ˈprɒdɪdʒi/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el prodigio

Meaning:  a young person with exceptional talent or ability

Example:  She was a child prodigy, playing piano at age four.

→  Ella fue un prodigio infantil, tocaba el piano a los cuatro años.

PROLIFERATE   /prəˈlɪfəreɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  proliferar

Meaning:  to increase rapidly in number

Example:  Online scams have proliferated in recent years.

→  Las estafas en línea han proliferado en los últimos años.

PROPITIATE   /prəˈpɪʃieɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  propiciar; aplacar (a alguien)

Meaning:  to make someone less angry by doing something to please them

Example:  They offered gifts to propitiate the offended neighbor.

→  Ofrecieron regalos para aplacar al vecino ofendido.

PROPONENT   /prəˈpoʊnənt/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el defensor; la defensora; el partidario

Meaning:  a person who publicly supports an idea or plan

Example:  She's a strong proponent of renewable energy.

→  Ella es una fuerte defensora de la energía renovable.

PROPRIETY   /prəˈpraɪəti/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el decoro; la corrección

Meaning:  conforming to accepted standards of behavior

Example:  He always behaved with great propriety at formal events.

→  Siempre se comportó con gran decoro en eventos formales.

PRUDENT   /ˈpruːdənt/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  prudente

Meaning:  careful and sensible, especially about money or risk

Example:  It would be prudent to save some money each month.

→  Sería prudente ahorrar algo de dinero cada mes.

PURVIEW   /ˈpɜːrvjuː/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el ámbito; la competencia

Meaning:  the range of someone's responsibility or authority

Example:  This decision falls outside my purview.

→  Esta decisión está fuera de mi ámbito de competencia.

QUELL   /kwɛl/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  reprimir; sofocar; calmar

Meaning:  to put an end to something, such as unrest or fear

Example:  The government sent troops to quell the uprising.

→  El gobierno envió tropas para sofocar el levantamiento.

QUIXOTIC   /kwɪkˈsɒtɪk/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  quijotesco/a

Meaning:  having unrealistic ideals, especially in a romantic or foolishly idealistic way

Example:  His quixotic plan to sail around the world alone worried his family.

→  Su plan quijotesco de navegar solo alrededor del mundo preocupó a su familia.

REBUKE   /rɪˈbjuːk/  •  verb; noun  •  C1

Spanish:  reprender; la reprimenda

Meaning:  to criticize someone sharply for something they did

Example:  She was rebuked for arriving late to the ceremony.

→  Fue reprendida por llegar tarde a la ceremonia.

RECONCILE   /ˈrɛkənsaɪl/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  reconciliar; conciliar

Meaning:  to make two people or ideas friendly or compatible again

Example:  It took years for the two brothers to reconcile.

→  Los dos hermanos tardaron años en reconciliarse.

RECOUNT   /rɪˈkaʊnt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  relatar; narrar

Meaning:  to tell someone about an event, usually in detail

Example:  She recounted the whole story to her friends.

→  Ella les relató toda la historia a sus amigos.

REFUTE   /rɪˈfjuːt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  refutar

Meaning:  to prove that a statement or theory is wrong

Example:  The new evidence refutes his original theory.

→  Las nuevas pruebas refutan su teoría original.

RELEGATE   /ˈrɛlɪɡeɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  relegar

Meaning:  to assign someone or something to a lower rank or position

Example:  He felt relegated to a minor role in the project.

→  Se sintió relegado a un papel menor en el proyecto.

REMINISCE   /ˌrɛmɪˈnɪs/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  rememorar; evocar recuerdos

Meaning:  to talk about happy memories from the past

Example:  We spent the evening reminiscing about our school days.

→  Pasamos la noche rememorando nuestros días de escuela.

REPATRIATE   /riːˈpeɪtrieɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  repatriar

Meaning:  to send someone back to their own country

Example:  The tourists were repatriated after the natural disaster.

→  Los turistas fueron repatriados tras el desastre natural.

REPRIMAND   /ˈrɛprɪmænd/  •  verb; noun  •  C1

Spanish:  reprender; la reprimenda

Meaning:  to formally criticize someone for a mistake

Example:  He was reprimanded by his manager for the error.

→  Su jefe lo reprendió por el error.

REPUDIATE   /rɪˈpjuːdieɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  repudiar; rechazar

Meaning:  to formally reject or refuse to accept something

Example:  She repudiated the accusations against her.

→  Ella rechazó las acusaciones en su contra.

RESOLUTE   /ˈrɛzəluːt/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  resuelto/a; decidido/a

Meaning:  determined and firm in purpose

Example:  She remained resolute despite the setbacks.

→  Ella siguió firme a pesar de los contratiempos.

RETRACT   /rɪˈtrækt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  retractarse; retirar (una declaración)

Meaning:  to withdraw a statement, offer, or claim

Example:  He later retracted his earlier statement.

→  Más tarde se retractó de su declaración anterior.

RETROFIT   /ˈrɛtroʊfɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  reacondicionar; modernizar

Meaning:  to add new equipment or technology to something older

Example:  The old building was retrofitted with solar panels.

→  El edificio antiguo fue reacondicionado con paneles solares.

REVERBERATE   /rɪˈvɜːrbəreɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  resonar; repercutir

Meaning:  to be repeated as an echo; to have continuing effects

Example:  The decision will reverberate throughout the industry for years.

→  La decisión repercutirá en toda la industria durante años.

RUE   /ruː/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  lamentar; arrepentirse de

Meaning:  to regret something deeply

Example:  He would rue the day he ignored her advice.

→  Se arrepentiría del día en que ignoró su consejo.

SABOTAGE   /ˈsæbətɑːʒ/  •  noun; verb  •  C1

Spanish:  el sabotaje; sabotear

Meaning:  deliberate damage or disruption; to deliberately damage or disrupt something

Example:  The delay was caused by an act of sabotage.

→  El retraso fue causado por un acto de sabotaje.

SAGACIOUS   /səˈɡeɪʃəs/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  sagaz; perspicaz

Meaning:  having good judgment and wisdom

Example:  Her sagacious advice saved the company from a costly mistake.

→  Su consejo sagaz salvó a la empresa de un costoso error.

SANGUINE   /ˈsæŋɡwɪn/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  optimista; confiado/a

Meaning:  optimistic, especially in a difficult situation

Example:  She remained sanguine about the company's future.

→  Ella se mantuvo optimista sobre el futuro de la empresa.

SATIATE   /ˈseɪʃieɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  saciar

Meaning:  to satisfy a need or desire fully

Example:  Nothing seemed to satiate his curiosity.

→  Nada parecía saciar su curiosidad.

SECLUDE   /sɪˈkluːd/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  aislar; recluir

Meaning:  to keep someone or yourself apart from others

Example:  She secluded herself in her studio to finish the painting.

→  Se aisló en su estudio para terminar el cuadro.

SHUN   /ʃʌn/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  evitar; rehuir

Meaning:  to deliberately avoid someone or something

Example:  After the scandal, he was shunned by former friends.

→  Tras el escándalo, sus antiguos amigos lo rehuyeron.

SKEW   /skjuː/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  sesgar; distorsionar

Meaning:  to distort or make something unfair or unbalanced

Example:  A small sample size can skew the results.

→  Una muestra pequeña puede sesgar los resultados.

SLANDER   /ˈslændər/  •  noun; verb  •  C1

Spanish:  la calumnia; calumniar

Meaning:  a false spoken statement that damages someone's reputation

Example:  He sued the newspaper for slander.

→  Demandó al periódico por calumnia.

STAID   /steɪd/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  serio/a; formal (aburrido)

Meaning:  serious, respectable, and rather dull

Example:  The town has a rather staid, old-fashioned atmosphere.

→  El pueblo tiene un ambiente bastante serio y anticuado.

STIFLE   /ˈstaɪfəl/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  reprimir; sofocar

Meaning:  to prevent something from happening or developing; to suppress a feeling

Example:  She stifled a yawn during the long meeting.

→  Ella reprimió un bostezo durante la larga reunión.

STIPULATE   /ˈstɪpjəleɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  estipular

Meaning:  to demand or specify something as part of an agreement

Example:  The contract stipulates a two-week notice period.

→  El contrato estipula un período de aviso de dos semanas.

STOIC   /ˈstoʊɪk/  •  adjective; noun  •  C1

Spanish:  estoico/a

Meaning:  enduring pain or hardship without complaint

Example:  He remained stoic throughout the difficult treatment.

→  Se mantuvo estoico durante todo el tratamiento difícil.

SUBJUGATE   /ˈsʌbdʒəɡeɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  subyugar; someter

Meaning:  to bring a group of people under control by force

Example:  The empire subjugated many nations over the centuries.

→  El imperio subyugó a muchas naciones a lo largo de los siglos.

SUBSUME   /səbˈsuːm/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  subsumir; incluir dentro de

Meaning:  to include something as part of a larger category or group

Example:  The small department was subsumed into a larger one.

→  El pequeño departamento fue absorbido por uno más grande.

SUCCUMB   /səˈkʌm/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  sucumbir; ceder

Meaning:  to give in to pressure, temptation, or illness

Example:  She finally succumbed to the temptation of dessert.

→  Finalmente sucumbió a la tentación del postre.

Common:  succumb to

SUPERFLUOUS   /suːˈpɜːrfluəs/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  superfluo/a

Meaning:  more than is needed or wanted; unnecessary

Example:  Some of the details in the report felt superfluous.

→  Algunos detalles del informe parecían superfluos.

SUPERSEDE   /ˌsuːpərˈsiːd/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  reemplazar; sustituir

Meaning:  to take the place of something older or outdated

Example:  This model supersedes the older version.

→  Este modelo reemplaza a la versión anterior.

SURMISE   /sərˈmaɪz/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  suponer; conjeturar

Meaning:  to guess something based on limited information

Example:  From her expression, I surmised she was disappointed.

→  Por su expresión, supuse que estaba decepcionada.

TANTALIZE   /ˈtæntəlaɪz/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  tentar; provocar (con algo deseable)

Meaning:  to torment someone by showing something desirable but out of reach

Example:  The smell of fresh bread tantalized us as we walked by.

→  El olor a pan recién hecho nos tentaba al pasar.

Note:  British spelling: "tantalise".

TEMPERAMENT   /ˈtɛmpərəmənt/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el temperamento

Meaning:  a person's natural way of thinking, behaving, or reacting

Example:  She has a calm, patient temperament.

→  Ella tiene un temperamento tranquilo y paciente.

TENACIOUS   /təˈneɪʃəs/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  tenaz

Meaning:  holding firmly to something; not giving up easily

Example:  She's a tenacious negotiator who never backs down.

→  Ella es una negociadora tenaz que nunca se rinde.

TENACITY   /təˈnæsəti/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la tenacidad

Meaning:  determination to keep trying, without giving up

Example:  He pursued his goal with remarkable tenacity.

→  Persiguió su objetivo con una tenacidad notable.

TENET   /ˈtɛnɪt/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el principio; el precepto

Meaning:  a principle or belief held by a group or person

Example:  Equality is a core tenet of the organization.

→  La igualdad es un principio fundamental de la organización.

TESTAMENT   /ˈtɛstəmənt/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el testimonio (prueba); el testamento

Meaning:  something that serves as proof or evidence of a quality or fact

Example:  Her success is a testament to years of hard work.

→  Su éxito es un testimonio de años de trabajo duro.

Common:  a testament to

THROES   /θroʊz/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  los estertores; en pleno proceso de

Meaning:  a period of intense pain, struggle, or difficulty (used in "in the throes of")

Example:  The country is in the throes of a major economic crisis.

→  El país está en pleno proceso de una gran crisis económica.

Common:  in the throes of

TIRADE   /ˈtaɪreɪd/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la diatriba; el discurso furioso

Meaning:  a long, angry speech of criticism

Example:  He launched into a tirade about poor customer service.

→  Se lanzó a una diatriba sobre el mal servicio al cliente.

TRANQUILITY   /træŋˈkwɪləti/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la tranquilidad

Meaning:  a state of calm and peace

Example:  They moved to the countryside for some tranquility.

→  Se mudaron al campo en busca de tranquilidad.

Note:  British spelling: "tranquillity".

TRANSCEND   /trænˈsɛnd/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  trascender

Meaning:  to go beyond the usual limits of something

Example:  Great music transcends language and culture.

→  La gran música trasciende el idioma y la cultura.

TRANSGRESS   /trænzˈɡrɛs/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  transgredir

Meaning:  to break a moral rule or law

Example:  He transgressed the boundaries set by the agreement.

→  Transgredió los límites establecidos por el acuerdo.

TRANSGRESSION   /trænzˈɡrɛʃən/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la transgresión

Meaning:  an act that breaks a rule or moral code

Example:  He apologized for his past transgressions.

→  Se disculpó por sus transgresiones pasadas.

TRIBUTE   /ˈtrɪbjuːt/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el homenaje; el tributo

Meaning:  something said, done, or given to show respect or admiration

Example:  The concert was a tribute to the late musician.

→  El concierto fue un homenaje al músico fallecido.

Common:  pay tribute to

TROPE   /troʊp/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el tópico; el recurso literario

Meaning:  a common or overused theme or device in literature and film

Example:  The film relies heavily on familiar genre tropes.

→  La película se apoya mucho en tópicos habituales del género.

TUMULT   /ˈtuːmʌlt/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el tumulto

Meaning:  a state of confusion, disorder, or noisy commotion

Example:  The country was in a state of political tumult.

→  El país estaba en un estado de tumulto político.

TUTELAGE   /ˈtuːtəlɪdʒ/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la tutela; el aprendizaje bajo guía

Meaning:  guidance or instruction given by a teacher or mentor

Example:  Under her tutelage, he became a skilled painter.

→  Bajo su tutela, se convirtió en un pintor hábil.

TYPIFY   /ˈtɪpɪfaɪ/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  tipificar; ser un ejemplo típico de

Meaning:  to be a typical example of something

Example:  This building typifies the architecture of the 1960s.

→  Este edificio tipifica la arquitectura de los años sesenta.

TYRANNY   /ˈtɪrəni/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la tiranía

Meaning:  cruel and unjust use of power over others

Example:  The people rose up against years of tyranny.

→  El pueblo se alzó contra años de tiranía.

UBIQUITOUS   /juːˈbɪkwɪtəs/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  ubicuo/a; omnipresente

Meaning:  seeming to be present everywhere at once

Example:  Smartphones have become ubiquitous in modern life.

→  Los smartphones se han vuelto omnipresentes en la vida moderna.

UNDERCUT   /ˌʌndərˈkʌt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  vender más barato que; socavar

Meaning:  to sell at a lower price than a competitor; to weaken something

Example:  The new store undercut all its local competitors.

→  La nueva tienda vendió más barato que todos sus competidores locales.

UNDERSCORE   /ˌʌndərˈskɔːr/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  subrayar; enfatizar

Meaning:  to emphasize the importance of something

Example:  The report underscores the need for urgent action.

→  El informe subraya la necesidad de una acción urgente.

UNRAVEL   /ʌnˈrævəl/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  desentrañar; desenredar

Meaning:  to investigate and solve a mystery; to come apart, like threads

Example:  Detectives slowly unraveled the truth behind the case.

→  Los detectives desentrañaron lentamente la verdad tras el caso.

UPHEAVAL   /ʌpˈhiːvəl/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la agitación; el trastorno

Meaning:  a sudden, violent change or disturbance

Example:  The country went through years of political upheaval.

→  El país pasó por años de agitación política.

USURP   /juːˈzɜːrp/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  usurpar

Meaning:  to take a position of power illegally or by force

Example:  He usurped the throne from his older brother.

→  Usurpó el trono a su hermano mayor.

UTOPIA   /juːˈtoʊpiə/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la utopía

Meaning:  an imagined perfect place or state of society

Example:  The novel describes a technological utopia.

→  La novela describe una utopía tecnológica.

VAGARY   /ˈveɪɡəri/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el capricho; la vicisitud

Meaning:  an unpredictable change or event, often used in the plural

Example:  Farmers must deal with the vagaries of the weather.

→  Los agricultores deben lidiar con los caprichos del clima.

VALOR   /ˈvælər/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el valor; la valentía

Meaning:  great courage, especially in battle

Example:  The soldier was honored for his valor.

→  El soldado fue condecorado por su valentía.

Note:  British spelling: "valour".

VANTAGE   /ˈvæntɪdʒ/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la posición ventajosa; el punto de vista

Meaning:  a place or position giving a good view or advantage

Example:  From this vantage point, you can see the whole valley.

→  Desde este punto de vista, se puede ver todo el valle.

Common:  vantage point

VEHEMENCE   /ˈviːəməns/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la vehemencia

Meaning:  strong, forceful feeling or expression

Example:  She denied the accusation with real vehemence.

→  Ella negó la acusación con verdadera vehemencia.

VENEER   /vəˈnɪr/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la apariencia (engañosa); el chapado

Meaning:  an outward appearance that hides the true nature of something; a thin decorative layer

Example:  Behind his friendly veneer, he was quite ruthless.

→  Detrás de su apariencia amistosa, era bastante despiadado.

VERACITY   /vəˈræsəti/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la veracidad

Meaning:  the quality of being true or accurate

Example:  Reporters questioned the veracity of his claims.

→  Los periodistas cuestionaron la veracidad de sus afirmaciones.

VERVE   /vɜːrv/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el brío; el entusiasmo vital

Meaning:  energy and enthusiasm, especially in artistic performance

Example:  She performed the piece with real verve.

→  Ella interpretó la pieza con verdadero brío.

VESTIGE   /ˈvɛstɪdʒ/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el vestigio

Meaning:  a small remaining trace of something that once existed

Example:  The old wall is a vestige of the town's medieval past.

→  El muro antiguo es un vestigio del pasado medieval del pueblo.

VEX   /vɛks/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  molestar; fastidiar

Meaning:  to annoy or worry someone

Example:  The unresolved question continued to vex the researchers.

→  La pregunta sin resolver seguía fastidiando a los investigadores.

VEXATION   /vɛkˈseɪʃən/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el fastidio; la contrariedad

Meaning:  a feeling of annoyance or frustration

Example:  He sighed in vexation at the delay.

→  Suspiró con fastidio por el retraso.

VICISSITUDE   /vɪˈsɪsɪtuːd/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la vicisitud

Meaning:  a change of circumstances, often unpleasant, usually used in the plural

Example:  They stayed together through all the vicissitudes of life.

→  Permanecieron juntos a través de todas las vicisitudes de la vida.

VILIFY   /ˈvɪlɪfaɪ/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  vilipendiar; difamar

Meaning:  to speak or write about someone in a very critical way

Example:  He was vilified in the press for his comments.

→  Fue vilipendiado en la prensa por sus comentarios.

VINDICATE   /ˈvɪndɪkeɪt/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  vindicar; reivindicar

Meaning:  to clear someone of blame, or to prove something was correct

Example:  The test results vindicated her original theory.

→  Los resultados de la prueba reivindicaron su teoría original.

VINDICATION   /ˌvɪndɪˈkeɪʃən/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la vindicación; la reivindicación

Meaning:  proof that someone was right or justified in what they did

Example:  Winning the award felt like a real vindication.

→  Ganar el premio se sintió como una verdadera reivindicación.

VINDICTIVE   /vɪnˈdɪktɪv/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  vengativo/a

Meaning:  having a strong desire for revenge

Example:  He became vindictive after losing the promotion.

→  Se volvió vengativo tras perder el ascenso.

VIRTUOSO   /ˌvɜːrtʃuˈoʊsoʊ/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el virtuoso; la virtuosa

Meaning:  a person with exceptional skill in an art, especially music

Example:  She's regarded as a piano virtuoso.

→  Se la considera una virtuosa del piano.

VISCERAL   /ˈvɪsərəl/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  visceral

Meaning:  based on deep feeling rather than reason

Example:  She had a visceral reaction to the disturbing footage.

→  Ella tuvo una reacción visceral ante las imágenes perturbadoras.

VITALITY   /vaɪˈtæləti/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la vitalidad

Meaning:  energy and enthusiasm for life

Example:  She has an incredible vitality for her age.

→  Ella tiene una vitalidad increíble para su edad.

VIVACITY   /vɪˈvæsəti/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la vivacidad

Meaning:  the quality of being lively and full of energy

Example:  Her vivacity made her the life of every party.

→  Su vivacidad la convertía en el alma de cada fiesta.

VOLATILITY   /ˌvɒləˈtɪləti/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la volatilidad

Meaning:  the tendency to change quickly and unpredictably

Example:  The market has shown a lot of volatility this year.

→  El mercado ha mostrado mucha volatilidad este año.

WANDERLUST   /ˈwɒndərlʌst/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  las ganas de viajar; el afán viajero

Meaning:  a strong desire to travel and explore the world

Example:  Her wanderlust took her to over forty countries.

→  Sus ganas de viajar la llevaron a más de cuarenta países.

WANE   /weɪn/  •  verb  •  C1

Spanish:  menguar; disminuir

Meaning:  to gradually become weaker or smaller

Example:  His interest in the project began to wane after a few months.

→  Su interés en el proyecto empezó a menguar después de unos meses.

WARINESS   /ˈwɛrinəs/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la cautela; la desconfianza

Meaning:  caution about possible danger or risk

Example:  There's a growing wariness about sharing data online.

→  Hay una creciente cautela sobre compartir datos en línea.

WISTFUL   /ˈwɪstfəl/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  melancólico/a; nostálgico/a

Meaning:  feeling or showing a vague or regretful longing

Example:  She gave a wistful smile as she remembered her childhood home.

→  Ella esbozó una sonrisa melancólica al recordar su casa de la infancia.

WISTFULNESS   /ˈwɪstfəlnəs/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  la melancolía; la nostalgia

Meaning:  a feeling of vague or regretful longing

Example:  There was a note of wistfulness in her voice.

→  Había una nota de melancolía en su voz.

ZEAL   /ziːl/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el celo; el entusiasmo fervoroso

Meaning:  great energy and enthusiasm for a cause or goal

Example:  She pursued her research with great zeal.

→  Ella persiguió su investigación con gran celo.

ZEALOT   /ˈzɛlət/  •  noun  •  C1

Spanish:  el fanático; la fanática

Meaning:  a person who is fanatically committed to a cause

Example:  He was seen as a zealot by more moderate colleagues.

→  Sus colegas más moderados lo veían como un fanático.

ZEALOUS   /ˈzɛləs/  •  adjective  •  C1

Spanish:  celoso/a (entusiasta); fervoroso/a

Meaning:  showing great energy and enthusiasm for a cause

Example:  He's a zealous supporter of local environmental projects.

→  Es un fervoroso partidario de los proyectos ambientales locales.

# Volume 1 Complete

That's all 2,000 words of Volume 1, every one with a full entry. The study tools that follow — a 30-day tracker, a review checklist, and pages for your most difficult words — are designed to help you actually learn this vocabulary, not just look it up.

Ready for words 2,001–4,000? Just ask to start Volume 2 — it will build on the finished word list in the Appendix so nothing is ever repeated.
